Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least not in a way that stands out to me. I mean, I can get stressed like anyone, but I don’t usually feel anxious when I’m just at home or off work. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious when you're not at work and there's no particular stressor or pressure?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Like if there’s no real reason—no work thing, no deadline or something—I don’t usually feel anxious out of nowhere. At least, I haven’t noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any problems with your memory?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I guess I have noticed that. I wouldn’t say I’m forgetting who people are or anything like that, but I forget things I really should remember—like calling someone back, or following up on something at work, like a missing order. At home it can be stuff like paying a bill, or I’ve even almost missed or missed appointments.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ll fully mean to do it, and sometimes I’ll even set a reminder, and then somehow it still slips past me. I know that probably sounds bad… but yes, that’s been happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least not in a way that stands out to me. I know that probably sounds bad… but yes, that’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or down most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not most of the time. Is that… does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sustaining focus on tasks when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Maybe not with every task, but if it’s something that needs a lot of focused mental effort, I tend to put it off.\n\nLike if I know I have to sit down and do a detailed chart review or audit documentation across shifts, I’ll sort of keep finding other things to do instead—bedside tasks, little things that feel more immediate. It’s not that I don’t care about the charting, it just feels really hard to make myself sit with it, like… almost like trying to climb a wall. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself daydreaming or getting lost in thought when you're not supposed to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there daydreaming all the time, exactly. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you. I guess the main thing is, at work, I’m having a really hard time keeping my train of thought. I’m a nurse, and on the floor there’s always something happening—an IV pump beeping, a call light, someone asking a question nearby—and I’ll suddenly realize I’ve completely lost what I was in the middle of thinking through, like a patient flow issue or what I was about to do next. I know it’s a busy environment, so maybe that’s normal to some extent, but it’s been happening enough that I thought I should come in and talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty in keeping your train of thought?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say the really noticeable, major version of it has been going on since I took on the expanded charge-nurse responsibilities, so almost two years now. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that’s when it started feeling like a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any difficulties with focusing or keeping your train of thought outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I guess I notice it sometimes, but the main place it really stands out is on the floor. Like, an IV pump beeps or a call light goes off, or even just someone nearby asking another nurse something, and I’ll suddenly lose the thread of what I was working through—like a patient flow issue—and I have to sort of backtrack. I’m not sure if that answers your question properly, but that’s the part that’s been most concerning to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're on the floor and these distractions happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly… maybe a bit overwhelmed in the moment, but it’s hard to separate that from just the floor being busy, you know? I don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like I get thrown off and then I’m trying to recover what I was doing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least not in a way that stands out to me. I mean, I can get stressed like anyone, but I don’t usually feel anxious when I’m just at home or off work. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious when you're not at work and there's no particular stressor or pressure?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Like if there’s no real reason—no work thing, no deadline or something—I don’t usually feel anxious out of nowhere. At least, I haven’t noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any problems with your memory?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I guess I have noticed that. I wouldn’t say I’m forgetting who people are or anything like that, but I forget things I really should remember—like calling someone back, or following up on something at work, like a missing order. Maybe not with every task, but if it’s something that needs a lot of focused mental effort, I tend to put it off.\n\nLike if I know I have to sit down and do a detailed chart review or audit documentation across shifts, I’ll sort of keep finding other things to do instead—bedside tasks, little things that feel more immediate. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself daydreaming or getting lost in thought when you're not supposed to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there daydreaming all the time, exactly. It’s more like I get pulled off track really easily.\n\nHonestly, that’s kind of the main reason I came in. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel frustrated or upset when you forget things or have trouble keeping your train of thought?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get frustrated. I try not to show it, especially at work, but it’s upsetting when I was just thinking through a patient flow issue and then something normal happens—like an IV pump beeping, or a call light, or someone asking another nurse a question nearby—and suddenly it’s just… gone.\n\nI feel kind of embarrassed saying that because I know those interruptions are part of the job. But that’s honestly the main reason I’m here. It feels like I lose my train of thought constantly, and then I’m annoyed with myself because I should be able to hold onto it better. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Like if there’s no real reason—no work thing, no deadline or something—I don’t usually feel anxious out of nowhere. At least, I haven’t noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any problems with your memory?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I guess I have noticed that. I wouldn’t say I’m forgetting who people are or anything like that, but I forget things I really should remember—like calling someone back, or following up on something at work, like a missing order. At home it can be stuff like paying a bill, or I’ve even almost missed or missed appointments.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ll fully mean to do it, and sometimes I’ll even set a reminder, and then somehow it still slips past me. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself daydreaming or getting lost in thought when you're not supposed to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there daydreaming all the time, exactly. Yeah… I mean, I don’t think I’m having big personal conflicts or anything like that. People are generally kind to me.

I know that probably sounds bad… but yes, that’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or down most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not most of the time. I mean, I have days where I feel kind of low or frustrated, but I wouldn’t describe myself as sad all the time. Is that… does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sustaining focus on tasks when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Maybe not with every task, but if it’s something that needs a lot of focused mental effort, I tend to put it off.\n\nLike if I know I have to sit down and do a detailed chart review or audit documentation across shifts, I’ll sort of keep finding other things to do instead—bedside tasks, little things that feel more immediate. It’s not that I don’t care about the charting, it just feels really hard to make myself sit with it, like… almost like trying to climb a wall. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself daydreaming or getting lost in thought when you're not supposed to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there daydreaming all the time, exactly. It’s more like I get pulled off track really easily.\n\nHonestly, that’s kind of the main reason I came in. On the floor, I’ll be working through some patient flow issue in my head, and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question near me, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I’ve completely lost what I was just doing or thinking about.\n\nI know hospitals are busy and everyone gets interrupted, so I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t handle that environment… but it feels like it happens to me constantly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel frustrated or upset when you forget things or have trouble keeping your train of thought?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get frustrated. I try not to show it, especially at work, but it’s upsetting when I was just thinking through a patient flow issue and then something normal happens—like an IV pump beeping, or a call light, or someone asking another nurse a question nearby—and suddenly it’s just… gone.\n\nI feel kind of embarrassed saying that because I know those interruptions are part of the job. But that’s honestly the main reason I’m here. It feels like I lose my train of thought constantly, and then I’m annoyed with myself because I should be able to hold onto it better. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself getting lost in thought when you're supposed to be focusing on something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t know if I’d call it “lost in thought” exactly, but I definitely lose the thread of what I’m supposed to be focusing on.\n\nLike on the floor, I’ll be working through a patient flow issue in my head, and then an IV pump beeps, or someone asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I’m just… blank. I’ve forgotten what I was working on.\n\nI know that probably happens to everyone in a hospital setting, so I don’t want to overstate it, but it feels like it happens to me constantly. That’s really the main reason I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with family, friends, or colleagues due to your difficulty with focusing or memory?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think I’m having big personal conflicts or anything like that. People are generally kind to me.\n\nBut at work, it has started affecting how people relate to me, I guess. I’m on formal supervision now, and some of the other nurses have to double-check parts of my charge-nurse follow-through. I know it’s probably meant to be supportive and safe, but it feels really humiliating.\n\nI’m used to being someone people can rely on, and lately I feel like I’m becoming more of a liability than a leader. Yes… unfortunately, I think it has. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because I’m still trying really hard and I do care a lot about doing my job well.

Like if there’s no real reason—no work thing, no deadline or something—I don’t usually feel anxious out of nowhere. At least, I haven’t noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any problems with your memory?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I guess I have noticed that. I wouldn’t say I’m forgetting who people are or anything like that, but I forget things I really should remember—like calling someone back, or following up on something at work, like a missing order. At home it can be stuff like paying a bill, or I’ve even almost missed or missed appointments.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ll fully mean to do it, and sometimes I’ll even set a reminder, and then somehow it still slips past me. I know that probably sounds bad… but yes, that’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or down most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not most of the time. I mean, I have days where I feel kind of low or frustrated, but I wouldn’t describe myself as sad all the time. Is that… does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sustaining focus on tasks when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Maybe not with every task, but if it’s something that needs a lot of focused mental effort, I tend to put it off.\n\nLike if I know I have to sit down and do a detailed chart review or audit documentation across shifts, I’ll sort of keep finding other things to do instead—bedside tasks, little things that feel more immediate. It’s not that I don’t care about the charting, it just feels really hard to make myself sit with it, like… almost like trying to climb a wall. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself daydreaming or getting lost in thought when you're not supposed to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there daydreaming all the time, exactly. It’s more like I get pulled off track really easily.\n\nHonestly, that’s kind of the main reason I came in. On the floor, I’ll be working through some patient flow issue in my head, and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question near me, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I’ve completely lost what I was just doing or thinking about.\n\nI know hospitals are busy and everyone gets interrupted, so I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t handle that environment… but it feels like it happens to me constantly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel frustrated or upset when you forget things or have trouble keeping your train of thought?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get frustrated. I try not to show it, especially at work, but it’s upsetting when I was just thinking through a patient flow issue and then something normal happens—like an IV pump beeping, or a call light, or someone asking another nurse a question nearby—and suddenly it’s just… gone.\n\nI feel kind of embarrassed saying that because I know those interruptions are part of the job. But that’s honestly the main reason I’m here. It feels like I lose my train of thought constantly, and then I’m annoyed with myself because I should be able to hold onto it better. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself getting lost in thought when you're supposed to be focusing on something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t know if I’d call it “lost in thought” exactly, but I definitely lose the thread of what I’m supposed to be focusing on.\n\nLike on the floor, I’ll be working through a patient flow issue in my head, and then an IV pump beeps, or someone asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I’m just… blank. I’ve forgotten what I was working on.\n\nI know that probably happens to everyone in a hospital setting, so I don’t want to overstate it, but it feels like it happens to me constantly. That’s really the main reason I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with family, friends, or colleagues due to your difficulty with focusing or memory?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think I’m having big personal conflicts or anything like that. People are generally kind to me.\n\nBut at work, it has started affecting how people relate to me, I guess. I’m on formal supervision now, and some of the other nurses have to double-check parts of my charge-nurse follow-through. I know it’s probably meant to be supportive and safe, but it feels really humiliating.\n\nI’m used to being someone people can rely on, and lately I feel like I’m becoming more of a liability than a leader. I hate saying that, but… yeah, that’s been the biggest relationship impact, if that counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and memory have significantly impacted your ability to perform your job or affect your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… unfortunately, I think it has. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because I’m still trying really hard and I do care a lot about doing my job well.\n\nBut at work it’s gotten serious enough that I’m on formal supervision now. And other nurses are having to double-check parts of my follow-through when I’m charge nurse. I know that’s probably the right thing for safety, and I’m grateful people are being careful, but it feels awful.\n\nIt makes me feel like I’m becoming a liability instead of someone who’s supposed to be leading. And that’s… really hard to admit.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’m a nurse, and on the floor there’s always something happening—an IV pump beeping, a call light, someone asking a question nearby—and I’ll suddenly realize I’ve completely lost what I was in the middle of thinking through, like a patient flow issue or what I was about to do next. I know it’s a busy environment, so maybe that’s normal to some extent, but it’s been happening enough that I thought I should come in and talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty in keeping your train of thought?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say the really noticeable, major version of it has been going on since I took on the expanded charge-nurse responsibilities, so almost two years now. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that’s when it started feeling like a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any difficulties with focusing or keeping your train of thought outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I guess I notice it sometimes, but the main place it really stands out is on the floor. Like, an IV pump beeps or a call light goes off, or even just someone nearby asking another nurse something, and I’ll suddenly lose the thread of what I was working through—like a patient flow issue—and I have to sort of backtrack. I’m not sure if that answers your question properly, but that’s the part that’s been most concerning to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're on the floor and these distractions happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly… maybe a bit overwhelmed in the moment, but it’s hard to separate that from just the floor being busy, you know? I don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like I get thrown off and then I’m trying to recover what I was doing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least not in a way that stands out to me. I mean, I can get stressed like anyone, but I don’t usually feel anxious when I’m just at home or off work. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious when you're not at work and there's no particular stressor or pressure?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any problems with your memory?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I guess I have noticed that. I wouldn’t say I’m forgetting who people are or anything like that, but I forget things I really should remember—like calling someone back, or following up on something at work, like a missing order. At home it can be stuff like paying a bill, or I’ve even almost missed or missed appointments.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ll fully mean to do it, and sometimes I’ll even set a reminder, and then somehow it still slips past me. Is that… does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sustaining focus on tasks when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Maybe not with every task, but if it’s something that needs a lot of focused mental effort, I tend to put it off.\n\nLike if I know I have to sit down and do a detailed chart review or audit documentation across shifts, I’ll sort of keep finding other things to do instead—bedside tasks, little things that feel more immediate. It’s not that I don’t care about the charting, it just feels really hard to make myself sit with it, like… almost like trying to climb a wall. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself daydreaming or getting lost in thought when you're not supposed to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there daydreaming all the time, exactly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel frustrated or upset when you forget things or have trouble keeping your train of thought?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get frustrated. I try not to show it, especially at work, but it’s upsetting when I was just thinking through a patient flow issue and then something normal happens—like an IV pump beeping, or a call light, or someone asking another nurse a question nearby—and suddenly it’s just… gone.\n\nI feel kind of embarrassed saying that because I know those interruptions are part of the job. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself getting lost in thought when you're supposed to be focusing on something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I think it’s been there for a long time… probably as far back as elementary school, honestly. I don’t want to blame everything on that, because I did okay in school in some ways, but my teachers would write things like I was bright but careless.

I was always losing worksheets or forgetting to turn in homework, even when I had done it. And my mom had to sit with me most nights to keep me on track, otherwise I’d just… not finish things or forget what I was supposed to be doing.

So I guess it didn’t start recently. It’s just become harder to cover up, maybe.

At home it can be stuff like paying a bill, or I’ve even almost missed or missed appointments.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ll fully mean to do it, and sometimes I’ll even set a reminder, and then somehow it still slips past me. I know that probably sounds bad… but yes, that’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or down most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not most of the time. I mean, I have days where I feel kind of low or frustrated, but I wouldn’t describe myself as sad all the time. Is that… does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sustaining focus on tasks when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Maybe not with every task, but if it’s something that needs a lot of focused mental effort, I tend to put it off.\n\nLike if I know I have to sit down and do a detailed chart review or audit documentation across shifts, I’ll sort of keep finding other things to do instead—bedside tasks, little things that feel more immediate. It’s not that I don’t care about the charting, it just feels really hard to make myself sit with it, like… almost like trying to climb a wall. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself daydreaming or getting lost in thought when you're not supposed to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there daydreaming all the time, exactly. It’s more like I get pulled off track really easily.\n\nHonestly, that’s kind of the main reason I came in. On the floor, I’ll be working through some patient flow issue in my head, and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question near me, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I’ve completely lost what I was just doing or thinking about.\n\nI know hospitals are busy and everyone gets interrupted, so I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t handle that environment… but it feels like it happens to me constantly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel frustrated or upset when you forget things or have trouble keeping your train of thought?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get frustrated. I try not to show it, especially at work, but it’s upsetting when I was just thinking through a patient flow issue and then something normal happens—like an IV pump beeping, or a call light, or someone asking another nurse a question nearby—and suddenly it’s just… gone.\n\nI feel kind of embarrassed saying that because I know those interruptions are part of the job. But that’s honestly the main reason I’m here. It feels like I lose my train of thought constantly, and then I’m annoyed with myself because I should be able to hold onto it better. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself getting lost in thought when you're supposed to be focusing on something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t know if I’d call it “lost in thought” exactly, but I definitely lose the thread of what I’m supposed to be focusing on.\n\nLike on the floor, I’ll be working through a patient flow issue in my head, and then an IV pump beeps, or someone asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I’m just… blank. I’ve forgotten what I was working on.\n\nI know that probably happens to everyone in a hospital setting, so I don’t want to overstate it, but it feels like it happens to me constantly. That’s really the main reason I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with family, friends, or colleagues due to your difficulty with focusing or memory?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think I’m having big personal conflicts or anything like that. People are generally kind to me.\n\nBut at work, it has started affecting how people relate to me, I guess. I’m on formal supervision now, and some of the other nurses have to double-check parts of my charge-nurse follow-through. I know it’s probably meant to be supportive and safe, but it feels really humiliating.\n\nI’m used to being someone people can rely on, and lately I feel like I’m becoming more of a liability than a leader. I hate saying that, but… yeah, that’s been the biggest relationship impact, if that counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and memory have significantly impacted your ability to perform your job or affect your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… unfortunately, I think it has. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because I’m still trying really hard and I do care a lot about doing my job well.\n\nBut at work it’s gotten serious enough that I’m on formal supervision now. And other nurses are having to double-check parts of my follow-through when I’m charge nurse. I know that’s probably the right thing for safety, and I’m grateful people are being careful, but it feels awful.\n\nIt makes me feel like I’m becoming a liability instead of someone who’s supposed to be leading. And that’s… really hard to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focusing and memory are causing you a lot of emotional distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I don’t want to sound dramatic, because I know work is stressful for everyone, especially in nursing, but this has been really upsetting.\n\nThe fact that it’s gotten to the point where I’m on formal supervision, and other nurses are having to double-check parts of my charge-nurse follow-through… it’s hard not to take that personally, even though I understand why it’s happening.\n\nI’m used to being someone people trust, and lately I feel like I’m becoming more of a liability than a leader. That’s probably the part that’s been causing me the most distress.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities outside of work that you've had to stop doing or significantly change because of your memory or focusing issues?\n\nPatient: I haven’t completely stopped anything, exactly… but I’ve had to change how I do a lot of normal life stuff, if that makes sense.\n\nI forget things that really should be routine, like calling a family member back, or paying a bill at home, or showing up for an appointment. And it’s embarrassing because I do mean to do them. Sometimes I’ll even set a reminder, and then somehow I still don’t follow through.\n\nSo I try to put extra reminders or write things down more, but it doesn’t always work. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but yes, it has affected daily things outside of work too.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with focus or memory for as long as you can remember, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there for a long time… probably as far back as elementary school, honestly. I don’t want to blame everything on that, because I did okay in school in some ways, but my teachers would write things like I was bright but careless.\n\nI was always losing worksheets or forgetting to turn in homework, even when I had done it. And my mom had to sit with me most nights to keep me on track, otherwise I’d just… not finish things or forget what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nSo I guess it didn’t start recently. It’s just become harder to cover up, maybe.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think I have, more than I’d like to admit. Not in a constant way, maybe, but I do lean on people to remind me about things that should be pretty routine.

Like my family will remind me to call someone back, or I’ll need a nudge about paying a bill or an appointment. Sometimes I’ve even set a reminder myself, and then I still somehow don’t actually follow through, which is frustrating and kind of embarrassing.

So yes, I guess I do rely on others more than I probably should… if that answers what you’re asking.

I wouldn’t say I’m forgetting who people are or anything like that, but I forget things I really should remember—like calling someone back, or following up on something at work, like a missing order. I know that probably sounds bad… but yes, that’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or down most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not most of the time. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel frustrated or upset when you forget things or have trouble keeping your train of thought?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get frustrated. I try not to show it, especially at work, but it’s upsetting when I was just thinking through a patient flow issue and then something normal happens—like an IV pump beeping, or a call light, or someone asking another nurse a question nearby—and suddenly it’s just… gone.\n\nI feel kind of embarrassed saying that because I know those interruptions are part of the job. But that’s honestly the main reason I’m here. It feels like I lose my train of thought constantly, and then I’m annoyed with myself because I should be able to hold onto it better. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself getting lost in thought when you're supposed to be focusing on something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t know if I’d call it “lost in thought” exactly, but I definitely lose the thread of what I’m supposed to be focusing on.\n\nLike on the floor, I’ll be working through a patient flow issue in my head, and then an IV pump beeps, or someone asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I’m just… blank. I’ve forgotten what I was working on.\n\nI know that probably happens to everyone in a hospital setting, so I don’t want to overstate it, but it feels like it happens to me constantly. That’s really the main reason I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with family, friends, or colleagues due to your difficulty with focusing or memory?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think I’m having big personal conflicts or anything like that. People are generally kind to me.\n\nBut at work, it has started affecting how people relate to me, I guess. I’m on formal supervision now, and some of the other nurses have to double-check parts of my charge-nurse follow-through. I know it’s probably meant to be supportive and safe, but it feels really humiliating.\n\nI’m used to being someone people can rely on, and lately I feel like I’m becoming more of a liability than a leader. I hate saying that, but… yeah, that’s been the biggest relationship impact, if that counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and memory have significantly impacted your ability to perform your job or affect your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… unfortunately, I think it has. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because I’m still trying really hard and I do care a lot about doing my job well.\n\nBut at work it’s gotten serious enough that I’m on formal supervision now. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, R41.0, F43.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi… thank you. I guess the main thing is, at work, I’m having a really hard time keeping my train of thought. I’m a nurse, and on the floor there’s always something happening—an IV pump beeping, a call light, someone asking a question nearby—and I’ll suddenly realize I’ve completely lost what I was in the middle of thinking through, like a patient flow issue or what I was about to do next. I know it’s a busy environment, so maybe that’s normal to some extent, but it’s been happening enough that I thought I should come in and talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been experiencing this difficulty in keeping your train of thought?\n\nPatient: It’s been… I’d say the really noticeable, major version of it has been going on since I took on the expanded charge-nurse responsibilities, so almost two years now. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but that’s when it started feeling like a real problem.\n\nDoctor: Are you noticing any difficulties with focusing or keeping your train of thought outside of work?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I guess I notice it sometimes, but the main place it really stands out is on the floor. Like, an IV pump beeps or a call light goes off, or even just someone nearby asking another nurse something, and I’ll suddenly lose the thread of what I was working through—like a patient flow issue—and I have to sort of backtrack. I’m not sure if that answers your question properly, but that’s the part that’s been most concerning to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or overwhelmed when you're on the floor and these distractions happen?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly… maybe a bit overwhelmed in the moment, but it’s hard to separate that from just the floor being busy, you know? I don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like I get thrown off and then I’m trying to recover what I was doing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… at least not in a way that stands out to me. I mean, I can get stressed like anyone, but I don’t usually feel anxious when I’m just at home or off work. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel anxious when you're not at work and there's no particular stressor or pressure?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. Like if there’s no real reason—no work thing, no deadline or something—I don’t usually feel anxious out of nowhere. At least, I haven’t noticed that. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any problems with your memory?\n\nPatient: Um… yeah, I guess I have noticed that. I wouldn’t say I’m forgetting who people are or anything like that, but I forget things I really should remember—like calling someone back, or following up on something at work, like a missing order. At home it can be stuff like paying a bill, or I’ve even almost missed or missed appointments.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is I’ll fully mean to do it, and sometimes I’ll even set a reminder, and then somehow it still slips past me. I know that probably sounds bad… but yes, that’s been happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or down most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not most of the time. I mean, I have days where I feel kind of low or frustrated, but I wouldn’t describe myself as sad all the time. Is that… does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble sustaining focus on tasks when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Maybe not with every task, but if it’s something that needs a lot of focused mental effort, I tend to put it off.\n\nLike if I know I have to sit down and do a detailed chart review or audit documentation across shifts, I’ll sort of keep finding other things to do instead—bedside tasks, little things that feel more immediate. It’s not that I don’t care about the charting, it just feels really hard to make myself sit with it, like… almost like trying to climb a wall. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself daydreaming or getting lost in thought when you're not supposed to be?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m just sitting there daydreaming all the time, exactly. It’s more like I get pulled off track really easily.\n\nHonestly, that’s kind of the main reason I came in. On the floor, I’ll be working through some patient flow issue in my head, and then an IV pump starts beeping, or someone asks another nurse a question near me, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I’ve completely lost what I was just doing or thinking about.\n\nI know hospitals are busy and everyone gets interrupted, so I don’t want to make it sound like I can’t handle that environment… but it feels like it happens to me constantly. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel frustrated or upset when you forget things or have trouble keeping your train of thought?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do get frustrated. I try not to show it, especially at work, but it’s upsetting when I was just thinking through a patient flow issue and then something normal happens—like an IV pump beeping, or a call light, or someone asking another nurse a question nearby—and suddenly it’s just… gone.\n\nI feel kind of embarrassed saying that because I know those interruptions are part of the job. But that’s honestly the main reason I’m here. It feels like I lose my train of thought constantly, and then I’m annoyed with myself because I should be able to hold onto it better. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself getting lost in thought when you're supposed to be focusing on something?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. I don’t know if I’d call it “lost in thought” exactly, but I definitely lose the thread of what I’m supposed to be focusing on.\n\nLike on the floor, I’ll be working through a patient flow issue in my head, and then an IV pump beeps, or someone asks another nurse a question, or a call light goes off, and suddenly I’m just… blank. I’ve forgotten what I was working on.\n\nI know that probably happens to everyone in a hospital setting, so I don’t want to overstate it, but it feels like it happens to me constantly. That’s really the main reason I came in.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any problems in your relationships with family, friends, or colleagues due to your difficulty with focusing or memory?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t think I’m having big personal conflicts or anything like that. People are generally kind to me.\n\nBut at work, it has started affecting how people relate to me, I guess. I’m on formal supervision now, and some of the other nurses have to double-check parts of my charge-nurse follow-through. I know it’s probably meant to be supportive and safe, but it feels really humiliating.\n\nI’m used to being someone people can rely on, and lately I feel like I’m becoming more of a liability than a leader. I hate saying that, but… yeah, that’s been the biggest relationship impact, if that counts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focusing and memory have significantly impacted your ability to perform your job or affect your relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… unfortunately, I think it has. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, because I’m still trying really hard and I do care a lot about doing my job well.\n\nBut at work it’s gotten serious enough that I’m on formal supervision now. And other nurses are having to double-check parts of my follow-through when I’m charge nurse. I know that’s probably the right thing for safety, and I’m grateful people are being careful, but it feels awful.\n\nIt makes me feel like I’m becoming a liability instead of someone who’s supposed to be leading. And that’s… really hard to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focusing and memory are causing you a lot of emotional distress?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I don’t want to sound dramatic, because I know work is stressful for everyone, especially in nursing, but this has been really upsetting.\n\nThe fact that it’s gotten to the point where I’m on formal supervision, and other nurses are having to double-check parts of my charge-nurse follow-through… it’s hard not to take that personally, even though I understand why it’s happening.\n\nI’m used to being someone people trust, and lately I feel like I’m becoming more of a liability than a leader. That’s probably the part that’s been causing me the most distress.\n\nDoctor: Are there any daily activities outside of work that you've had to stop doing or significantly change because of your memory or focusing issues?\n\nPatient: I haven’t completely stopped anything, exactly… but I’ve had to change how I do a lot of normal life stuff, if that makes sense.\n\nI forget things that really should be routine, like calling a family member back, or paying a bill at home, or showing up for an appointment. And it’s embarrassing because I do mean to do them. Sometimes I’ll even set a reminder, and then somehow I still don’t follow through.\n\nSo I try to put extra reminders or write things down more, but it doesn’t always work. I’m not sure if that’s the kind of thing you mean, but yes, it has affected daily things outside of work too.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with focus or memory for as long as you can remember, or did they start more recently?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there for a long time… probably as far back as elementary school, honestly. I don’t want to blame everything on that, because I did okay in school in some ways, but my teachers would write things like I was bright but careless.\n\nI was always losing worksheets or forgetting to turn in homework, even when I had done it. And my mom had to sit with me most nights to keep me on track, otherwise I’d just… not finish things or forget what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nSo I guess it didn’t start recently. It’s just become harder to cover up, maybe.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to rely more heavily on others to help you remember things or complete tasks outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have, more than I’d like to admit. I mean… I’ve wondered about it, and I think other people close to me have sort of hinted at it before, but I’ve never actually been diagnosed or anything.\n\nI guess I wasn’t sure if it was really that, or if I just needed to be more organized. I don’t want to overstate it. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
